    The white pawn is at e5.

    Wait, it attacks the pawn at h3? No, f5 to h3 is not a diagonal. f5 to g4 then h3? No.
    f5 is on a light square. h3 is on a light square.
    f5 -> g4 -> h3. Yes, it is a diagonal!
    So `d7f5` attacks the pawn on h3.

    d4: col 3, row 4.

    It moves the bishop to a central square.

    d7: col 3, row 1.
    f5: col 5, row 3.

    4. If White plays d4, it blocks the bishop on d3.

    `d7f5` puts a bishop on a good square and attacks h3 (which is defended by the rook on h1).

    Yes, the rook is at h1, the pawn is at h3. That's the same file.
    So `d7f5` threatens h3, but `Rxh3` is possible if the bishop captures it.
    Wait, the rook at h1 *defends* h3? No, the rook is *behind* the pawn.
    The rook is at h1 and the pawn is at h3. The rook cannot capture a piece at h3 unless that piece is an opponent's piece.

    Yes, the rook at h1 is at (row 7, col 7), the bishop would be at (row 5, col 7).
    That's a vertical move. So `Rxh3` is possible.

    If Black plays `d7xh3`, the bishop is now on h3. The rook on h1 is now attacking the bishop at h3.

    It attacks e5. This is an active move that challenges white's central pawn.

    `c5d4` - Centralizes the bishop.
    `g7g6` - Solidifies the kingside.

    `f7f6` looks like the most proactive and beneficial move. It challenges e5 and prepares to potentially open the f-file or create a pawn weakness.

    It's a very solid move.
